* China’s AI Acceleration
  Foreign Policy | 06 Aug 20:33 UTC
  Washington isn’t sure how to counter Chinese technological advancements.
  FULL TEXT: Welcome back to Foreign Policy ’s Situation Report, where our team has spent the last week taking turns falling sick. But don’t worry, we rallied so that we could bring you this week’s edition. All right, here’s what’s on tap for the day: A Chinese AI conundrum in Washington, another potential Hormuz reopening , and the cost of Trump’s new battleships. Welcome back to Foreign Policy ’s Situation Report, where our team has spent the last week taking turns falling sick. But don’t worry, we rallied so that we could bring you this week’s edition. All right, here’s what’s on tap for the day: A Chinese AI conundrum in Washington, another potential Hormuz reopening , and the cost of Trump’s new battleships. No, we’re not talking about the latest U.S. attempt to reach a deal with Iran (though there’s more on that below). Instead, we’re talking about Washington’s latest freakout over China’s artificial intelligence capabilities. Many people ( us included ) have spent years asking the question of which of the world’s two biggest economies is winning the AI race. The latest round of soul-searching has been sparked by a model named Kimi that was created by Chinese company Moonshot AI. The chatbot’s latest version, which released in July, showed capabilities that potentially rival the top U.S. models. The prevailing wisdom indicates a U.S. lead at the cutting edge through Silicon Valley giants such as OpenAI and Anthropic. But China’s bigger innovation may be in how Kimi, DeepSeek, and its other models are taking over the market. Most top Chinese models are open-weight, meaning that anyone can download their building blocks of data and code to create bespoke versions of the model at a much lower cost. As Rishi wrote soon after Kimi’s new model launched, it’s a form of Chinese AI-maxxing that gives countries around the world an easier entry point into an AI ecosystem than the far more expensive (and increasingly unreliable ) tech stack that Washington is exporting. The choices that countries in Africa are making, for example, are particularly instructive. According to a new report in the New York Times , developers across Kenya, Uganda, Ghana, and Nigeria are increasingly opting for Chinese AI models over U.S. ones to build everything from business and legal tools to education applications. Even U.S. startups are largely hooked on Chinese open-weight models, which resulted in a rather quick end to the Trump administration’s debate over whether they should be banned. Tru
  https://foreignpolicy.com/2026/08/06/trump-ai-china-kimi-anthropic-openai/

Israel is heading into what may be the most pivotal election in its history, but at stake this time isn’t so much its conflict with neighboring countries or its relations with the Palestinians. The core issue in this vote, slated for Oct. 27, is whether Israel will remain the liberal democracy its founders envisioned when it was established in 1948, or move even more vigorously toward a kind of illiberal regime infused by Orthodox Judaism. After presiding over a series of center-right governments for 12 years, Israeli Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu took an abrupt rightward shift when he returned to power in 2022 in a coalition with far-right and ultra-Orthodox (Haredi) parties. It soon emerged that his government had two primary goals. The first was to undercut the powers of an independent judiciary and vastly increase those of elected politicians, a plan quietly built on the assumption that, as in the case in other illiberal democracies, those in power would remain so because elections would cease to be entirely free and fair. The second was to give a greater role to the Jewish religion in the public space and provide massive financial, legal, and moral support for West Bank settlers. It was accompanied by ever-bigger government handouts and increased privileges for the Haredi community, most prominently a (so far failed) effort to enshrine in law the ultra-Orthodox’s traditional exemption from military service.   FULL TEXT: Understanding the conflict four years on. The Black Sea’s trade corridors are at the center of the Russia-Ukraine war again, as they were from 2022 to 2023, when a Russian naval blockade stopped Ukrainian maritime exports and shook agricultural markets worldwide. The current crisis looks similar, but it’s unique in important ways, as are the alternatives to resolve it. On July 23, Ukraine announced that maritime transport to and from its three unoccupied Black Sea ports was effectively frozen. Russian missile and drone attacks in July alone targeted 57 Ukrainian and foreign-flagged merchant ships in ports and at sea, including Golden Leo , a Turkish-owned bulk cargo carrier sailing under the flag of Guinea-Bissau. Three Russian missiles struck it in open water off the coast of Odesa on July 19, killing 10 people; the ship sank on July 27. By then, this year’s strikes had already cut Ukraine’s grain exports by roughly a third and doubled shipping freight costs into and out of the country. * How War Has Redefined Marriage in Gaza

  FULL TEXT: Next to rubble and pictures of the deceased, people in Gaza are still getting married. Athar Abu Samra reports on why couples are refusing to delay their nuptials. With so much already lost, and so much still under threat, families in Gaza are seizing moments of joy wherever they can. Marriage ceremonies are no longer what they once were—nor do they express hope in quite the same way—but they have become powerful testaments to resilience. As I worked on this story, I came to understand that the war has not only transformed weddings in Gaza. It has transformed people’s relationship with time itself. Before the war, marriage followed a familiar sequence. People finished their education, found work, built or rented a home, saved enough money and only then began planning a wedding. Families often postponed marriages until those expectations had been met. But now no one has that luxury — the luxury of time. I spoke with one mother who had lost several of her children during the war, yet hurried to arrange the marriage of her youngest son. She told me she had always dreamed, like any mother, of watching all of her children grow up, get married, and fill her home with grandchildren. Neither the victims nor their families were granted any measure of justice against the perpetrators who had killed their sons. This investigation by SIRAJ reveals for the first time the names and faces of two officers of the former regime’s army accused of being responsible for the Wadi Tuaim massacre, in which 13 people have been confirmed killed, nine of them from a single family, including a child.   FULL TEXT: Governments that deploy internet shutdowns to thwart protesters have struggled to clamp down on offline messaging apps. Bitchat, launched by the Twitter co-founder in 2025, allows nearby phones to communicate over Bluetooth even when mobile networks or internet access are unavailable. Last month, the app had its biggest real-world test after authorities shut down internet access during student protests in New Delhi. As protesters turned to Bitchat, the Indian government tried to block access to the app’s source code on GitHub, a Microsoft-owned platform that allows developers to create, store, manage, and share their code. In the past, the Indian government has blocked apps from official app stores due to various reasons, but digital rights advocates say this appears to be the first known attempt to geoblock an open-source software repository. The episode highlights a growing challenge for governments. Across the globe, state-enforced internet blackouts have become a primary weapon against protests and independent journalism during political crises. Beyond silencing dissent, these measures serve to block real-time documentation and evade international scrutiny of official misconduct. Apps built to function without the internet make those tactics less effective. “The decentralized architecture of the app means that there is no central server or database and therefore there cannot be a central kill switch,” Namrata Maheshwari, senior policy counsel and encryption policy lead at digital civil rights nonprofit Access Now, told Rest of World . “Downloaded apps will continue to work as long as they are within the physical proximity required for the mesh network to function.” Bitchat reached an all-time high of 430,000 daily active users in India on July 26, according to market intelligence firm Sensor Tower. Between July 20 and July 26, India accounted for 74% of the app’s global downloads. During Hong Kong’s 2014 protests, FireChat became a popular way for demonstrators to communicate without mobile networks. More recently, Bitchat downloads spiked during protests in Myanmar, Nepal, Uganda, Madagascar, and Iran. The app also reached the top of Apple’s App Store in Jamaica amid power outages during a hurricane last year. India’s response, however, went beyond targeting the app itself. The time-zone gap became an advantage: a surgeon in Boston could dictate before bed, and wake up to flawless notes typed in Bengaluru. By the early 2000s, the industry seemed secure. Companies built training academies, invested in IT systems and promised young recruits that this was a career with a future. But when I met Aakash (name changed) in June 2024, that promise had already unravelled. At 25, he was clinging to a short-term contract in a Bengaluru firm. He had joined a year earlier, after months of training. During recruitment, he asked the CEO whether AI posed a threat. ‘At least five years away,’ came the reassurance. Yet, by March 2024, as the financial year closed, American clients began to vanish. Contracts were cancelled or shifted to automated platforms. Hundreds of jobs disappeared almost overnight. The cafeteria that once buzzed at 3 a.m. emptied out. Hiring froze; the recruitment team itself was laid off. New employees were brought in under “conditional retention training,” only to be dismissed weeks later (an accounting trick to show inflated headcounts to prospective clients) and workers lived in limbo. Economists have a phrase for jobs like Aakash’s: high exposure , low complementarity. Which means that these are roles where machines can easily take over, rather than raising the human’s capacity to work. In Manila, tens of thousands of Filipino transcriptionists have already been displaced . In Nairobi, call center operators now compete with chatbots for contracts that once fed families. In Colombia, customer support roles vanish into generative systems. The pattern is the same: codified knowledge that once built middle-class livelihoods is being swallowed whole by automation. Even the people training the systems feel the blade at their neck. Contractors working on products like Gemini and AI Overviews describe a familiar dread: they are annotating their own obsolescence. Unlike earlier waves of mechanization that chipped away at manual labour, these systems target the cognitive core of professional life. The scale of this change is huge. Karim Khan expanded the court’s reach with some of its most consequential cases, including the arrest warrant issued for Putin and the case against Netanyahu. But his departure has created another crisis for an institution already struggling with questions about its credibility. The next prosecutor will inherit a court under unprecedented political pressure. The United States and Israel have intensified efforts to undermine the institution, while some member states have questioned whether the court can continue to function without stronger political support. At the same time, millions of victims of atrocities around the world continue to see the ICC as a necessary check on governments that fail to hold their own leaders accountable. The choice of chief prosecutor will be important, but it will not resolve the deeper questions facing the institution, which revolve around whether the ICC can maintain its independence while operating in a world shaped by power politics. For some observers, however, the attacks against the ICC are not evidence of failure. * A Haitian American Reflects on the Future

  The Trump administration is ending the program that allows many Haitians to live and work in the U.S. A Haitian American reflects on the damage the move will do to communities, the economy and the country’s ethos — and the underlying hostility that appears to have guided Trump’s decision. The post A Haitian American Reflects on the Future...
  FULL TEXT: Jan. 13, 2010, is a day that I will never forget. That Wednesday morning, I had several missed calls, text messages and notifications on my phone. I did not know it at the time, but the day before, a massive earthquake had struck near the capital city of Haiti, Port-au-Prince. As the child of Haitian immigrants who came to the United States in 1980, I have always kept up with events in Haiti, and this was, without question, the most devastating natural disaster that had occurred on the island in my lifetime. Hundreds of thousands of Haitians died as a result of the magnitude 7 earthquake, and millions more were severely impacted. At that point, I didn’t know all this — I was concerned for my family. Many of my cousins, uncles and aunts were still in Haiti. Were they okay? What would they do now that the capital had been damaged so severely? Where would they find refuge? On Jan. 15, in the midst of the chaos and confusion, the United States government extended temporary protected status (TPS) to Haitian nationals, giving them the ability to live and work in the United States without fear of deportation. Now, after 15 years of Haitians building their lives, integrating into their local communities and contributing to the economic and social fabric of the United States, the second Trump administration has abruptly ended TPS for Haiti and has plunged communities across the nation into a state of fear, anxiety and despair. Our industries, our hospitals, our nursing homes, our businesses and the entire U.S. economy will be harmed by this decision. TPS was established with the passage of the Immigration Act of 1990. The law significantly reshaped our immigration system and provided additional pathways for immigrants to come to the U.S. It raised the number of immigrants allowed to enter each year from 290,000 to 675,000 and introduced various programs, such as the Diversity Visa lottery and the H-1B Skilled Worker Visa Program, to increase the number of diverse, skilled workers entering the country. Along with these changes to the U.S. immigration system, this law established the TPS program, which, in this instance, provided work permits and protection for Haitians living in the U.S. after the earthquake. TPS designations are applied to countries that are experiencing extremely challenging circumstances, such as “on-going armed conflict, an environmental disaster, or extraordinary and temporary conditions,” as stated by the U.S. Department of Homeland Security.

* Magical Mystery Toads
  Longreads | 04 Aug 10:00 UTC
  The long, tangled, and mostly wrong history of the psychedelic toad.
  FULL TEXT: Kimon de Greef | Longreads | August 4, 2026 | 4,741 words (17 minutes) From The Ego Trip: Psychedelic Toads, a Trail of Deaths, and the Guru Who Peddled Transcendence by Kimon de Greef. Copyright © 2026 by Kimon de Greef. Excerpted by permission of Doubleday, an imprint of The Knopf Doubleday Group, a division of Penguin Random House LLC. All rights reserved. In 1935, two chemists at Tokyo’s Imperial University synthesized a new molecule and unwittingly launched one of the strangest narratives of the psychedelic age. Toshio Hoshino and Kenya Shimodaira were working on a family of compounds known as the tryptamines, which occur widely in nature and have a range of intriguing biological properties. The neurotransmitter serotonin, which among other things regulates mood, cognition, and social behavior, is a tryptamine. So are many psychoactive drugs, although this would only be discovered later on. The Japanese scientists had recently completed the world’s first laboratory synthesis of a molecule called bufotenine, which is produced by toads and certain species of mushrooms. Now, via a sequence of reactions, they engineered a close chemical relative. Diagrams of the two tryptamines looked nearly identical, each represented by two angular rings and a tail, resembling microscopic organisms; the only difference lay in the shape of the head. Such tiny changes in chemical structure “can lead to radically different consequences, like a railroad’s switch points,” as the writer and organic chemist Primo Levi once wrote. The new molecule’s name, following scientific convention, would be 5-MeO-DMT. Hoshino and Shimodaira published their findings in the Bulletin of the Chemical Society of Japan, leaving no explanation as to why they had conducted these experiments. The early 20th century was a time of wide-ranging exploration in organic chemistry, with researchers synthesizing new compounds to assess their pharmacological potential or better understand the reactions by which one substance could be transformed into another. Many of these compounds had no evident utility, such as 5-MeO-DMT, which for decades was thought only to be a product of the laboratory. This changed in 1959, when the molecule was isolated from a shrub native to Brazil. Not long afterward, 5-MeO-DMT was detected in Anadenanthera peregrina, a leguminous tree from the Caribbean and South America. For thousands of years, Indigenous communities had been harvesting its slender bean pods to prepare snuffs

Referencing one of the principal antagonists in the first “Godfather” film, he says, “The [Virgil] Sollozzo character in real life more likely would have had second, perhaps even thirdhand connections to the opium fields. The people who would run those opium fields, they could have been Kurdish, they could have been Turkish. The people who brought the refined morphine could have been Armenian, they could have been Lebanese, most likely Lebanese Christians. And the people who actually brought him the actual heroin, the dope, most likely would’ve been French.” Gingeras and Al Yafai’s discussion begins with American mafia culture, but expands both geographically and thematically, taking in how networks like those depicted on screen helped to build and shape the modern nations in which they now operate. Gingeras himself, an expert in Ottoman history, came to realize the personal impact of such networks when travelling to Turkey for work. “It took me leaving not so much New York, but leaving the United States and coming to Turkey to figure out that this was something that was a bigger story,” Gingeras says. “People all over the world struggled with this idea of, ‘What does the world mean to me when mafias are such an important part of it?’ In New York, it’s second nature, but what we, especially in the United States, oftentimes fail to really grasp is that this is something people struggle with everywhere.” Despite the fact they operate in the underworld, mafias matter to people everywhere, Gingeras argues, because they bring into relief the blurred edges of the nation-state. “Mafias are people with faces,” Gingeras says.

* Why Silicon Valley is divided over China’s powerful, cheap AI models
  Rest of World | 03 Aug 10:00 UTC
  Low-cost Chinese open-weight models are splitting tech executives and Washington over national security and competitiveness.
  FULL TEXT: The surge of powerful Chinese open-weight AI models has sparked a feud in Silicon Valley and Washington over whether they are threats to U.S. national security or essential parts of the expanding AI economy. Entrepreneurs and politicians are taking sides — arguing for either unfettered access or a strict crackdown — by forming alliances, issuing open letters, and launching social media volleys. While the leading American AI companies have kept their best models closed-source, Chinese AI labs have been releasing open-weight models as a way to attract global customers. These open models are becoming increasingly powerful. Moonshot’s open-weight model, Kimi K3, now ranks fourth on Artificial Analysis’ intelligence index , behind only Anthropic’s Opus 5 and Fable 5 and OpenAI’s GPT-5.6 Sol. While the adoption of Chinese models is on the rise in the U.S., politicians and companies have also sounded alarms. Some accuse Chinese companies of building powerful models with smuggled chips and distillation — using the output of American models to train their own. Others warn of the security risks posed by the powerful, free-to-download models. Whether to embrace or reject these Chinese models has become a divisive issue in the U.S. tech industry. Chinese open models are cost-effective. Open-weight models are free to download, but running them incurs computing costs. Many users pay to access the models through their developers or third-party cloud providers. Facing ballooning AI costs, some U.S. companies and developers have been diverting simpler tasks to Chinese models to save money. Companies can also use Chinese open-weight models to build customized AI systems, or host the models locally so they don’t have to share data with external parties. Chinese open models benefit AI infrastructure providers and companies that need affordable AI services. Nvidia has been a leading voice in support of open-weight models. The availability of open models creates a bigger customer base for its AI chips. On July 24, Nvidia chief executive Jensen Huang posted on X for the first time to share an open letter stressing the importance of open models in expanding access to the AI economy. Other tech giants, including Microsoft, Google, and Meta, have since joined the letter. OpenAI, which has released open-weight models that are smaller than China’s models, also signed on. “i want the US to win in AI both in open source and proprietary models,” chief executive Sam Altman wrote in reply

But the Israeli government seems to be obstructing efforts to carry out such tests, let alone assist the current and future victims of this environmental catastrophe. Aid workers and others entering Gaza attend an official briefing where they are informed that it is forbidden to leave the enclave with even small amounts of soil or sand. Once in the Strip, any scientists surreptitiously collecting research material risk being spotted by overhead surveillance drones. At departure checkpoints every piece of luggage is emptied onto a conveyor belt, and border guards carefully inspect each item. * How India’s Gen Z Made Profanity Political
  New Lines | 31 Jul 10:35 UTC
  India’s Gen Z is rewriting the rules of public speech. As profanity moves from private jokes and internet culture into protests, the backlash reveals a deeper generational divide over authenticity and respectability. The post How India’s Gen Z Made Profanity Political appeared first on New Lines Magazine .
  FULL TEXT: During the recent youth uprising in India, some profanities and slurs that most Indians would hesitate to repeat made their way into a handful of popular slogans aimed at Prime Minister Narendra Modi. They shocked not only the older generations, but also some millennials, as well as seasoned members of civil society, the independent media and leaders of progressive rights movements, accustomed to a certain decorum and seriousness in protest language. A popular slogan rhymed the Hindi word for “bitter” with the Marathi word for “pimp”: “Neem ka patta kadwa hai, Modi sala bhadwa hai” (“The neem leaf is bitter, Modi is a pimp”). In others, crude Hindi words for penis and buttocks were used. There was also some unsubtle wordplay on the English word “dictator.” Profanities appeared in graffiti on walls and police barricades around the central district of the city, especially after the police brutality that unfolded on July 20. Some protesters also played an audio of Modi reciting a Sanskrit verse on righteousness and moral duty to poke fun at him. To them, his pronunciation of the Sanskrit phrase “lavanena bhojyam” (“food is tasty because of salt”) sounded like a crude term for a penis. A popular meme on Instagram for a couple of years, it made its way to the streets alongside many other old and new memes. The recent protests were initiated by the satirical Cockroach Janata Party. The ruling Bharatiya Janata Party’s (BJP) social media troll networks and the mainstream Indian media that toes the government’s line have previously vilified protesters and critics of the Modi government, but failed to portray this movement as the handiwork of foreign agents, because most protesters belonged to the mainstream Hindu middle class, the BJP’s core support base. Only after the protests ended with the resignation of Education Minister Dharmendra Pradhan, a move aimed to contain the damage to Modi’s carefully crafted image after a decade in power, did a new line of attack emerge: discrediting the movement retroactively over the profanities used against Modi in its slogans, especially by young women. Shashi Shekhar Vempati, chairperson of the country’s Central Board of Film Certification, popularly known as the censor board, raised the issue of “mouthing of gender-demeaning profanities” in a post on X. So did Kanwal Sibal, a former diplomat, who took offence at what “young girls” were saying against Modi — language, he said, “only the most uncouth males would employ.” Kangan
  https://newlinesmag.com/spotlight/how-indias-gen-z-made-profanity-political/

* The Tumultuous History Behind Chicago’s Transformation Into a Quantum Metropolis
  New Lines | 31 Jul 09:42 UTC
  Chicago’s transformation from a frontier outpost to a modern metropolis was shaped by dramatic human and environmental ruptures. Three books retelling different aspects of the city’s history can help us grapple with its tense present, and its future in the emerging “quantum” economy. The post The Tumultuous History Behind Chicago’s Transf...
  FULL TEXT: “Till between the waters and the wind, came the marked-down derelicts with the dollar signs for eyes,” Nelson Algren wrote in his 1951 prose poem “Chicago: City on the Make.” “Looking for any prairie portage at all that hadn’t yet built a jail. Beside any old secondhand sea.” As Algren signaled, the story of Chicago is the pursuit of profit at the portage — the convergence of land, waterway and human anchorage: a site of collisions between landscapes, governments and people from all over the globe. This history raises crucial questions: What do we retain from the breathtaking transformation of a prairie into an embodiment of industry, architecture, urban modernism and public landscaping? What great human and environmental ruptures occurred amid the astonishing material and economic convulsions that forged this city in a garden? These questions are at the center of Ann Durkin Keating’s “Rising Up From Indian Country: The Battle of Fort Dearborn and the Birth of Chicago,” William Cronon’s “Nature’s Metropolis: Chicago and the Great West” and James Green’s “Death in the Haymarket: A Story of Chicago, the First Labor Movement and the Bombing That Divided Gilded Age America.” These historical studies help us make sense of Chicago today, as it stands at the threshold of artificial intelligence and under the boot of Immigration and Customs Enforcement (ICE). They tell a story of dramatic industrial upheaval, political realignments, labor conflict and environmental metamorphosis. Like many stories in American history, this one begins with the expulsion of Native people. At the center of Keating’s 2012 book is “the transfer of the western Great Lakes from Indian to American control through a combination of treaties and military conquest.” Chicago as we know it today, she writes, “would not exist without this conquest and the end of the Indian Country in the western Great Lakes.” Her book examines how an outpost for hunting and trading on open land dominated by populous Indian nations, fur traders of European descent and their dual-heritage offspring turned into Chicago, the city that had become a juggernaut of global capitalism by the time it hosted the World’s Columbian Exposition in 1893. The fur trader John Kinzie has been lionized as the white man whose family created a beachhead of civilization out of which an American city could develop.
